- Continuous awnings must be provided along streets with high pedestrian activity and active frontages. Awnings are to be consistent with areas identified for active frontage in the relevant Urban Design Concept Plan for the Centre (Figures 4 & 6).
- Awnings are to be designed with:
- A soffit height of 3.6m above the finished ground floor level.
- On sloping sites, awning soffit height may vary from a minimum of 3.2m and maximum of 4.0m.
- Awnings are to:
- be well-located.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 27 -->

- integrate with the overall design.
- consider interfaces with residential windows, balconies, mature street tree canopies, landscaping, power poles and street infrastructure.
- have a design, height, depth, form, and finish that complement the existing built form and street character.
- provide protection from sun and rain for pedestrians as well as reduction direct sunlight onto windows in summer.
- wrap around the secondary frontages of corner sites (Figure 6).
- be over building entries for building address and public domain amenity.
- consider retractable awnings in areas without an established pattern.
- provide adequate space to support street trees canopy growth.
- integrate with and conceal gutters and downpipes.
- provide lighting for pedestrian safety under awnings ( Figure 5).

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 28 -->

![page 28 img 1 (260x166)](images/wilton-development-control-plan-2021/p0028-87679fd20e2c.png)


*Figure 5. Lighting over footpaths.*

![page 28 img 2 (360x357)](images/wilton-development-control-plan-2021/p0028-3c441ead8b7c.png)


*Figure 6. Continuous and wrap-around awnings.*


<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 29 -->


# 2.9 Public domain (streetscape and public spaces)


## 2.9.1 Objectives

- Incorporate landscaping that softens the visual impact of buildings into the design of Centres and contributes to environmentally sensitive design of the development.
- Provide and support street trees and deep-soil landscaping in Centres to enable trees to reach mature heights and contribute to Council's 40% canopy cover target (Figure 8).
- Design and maintain good access for walking and cycling in Centres (Figure 7).
- Activate streets, enhance visual interest, and deliver an engaging pedestrian experience.
- Retain and strengthen important scenic and cultural views in the area.
- Ensure streets are shaded and protected from natural elements to encourage safe and walkable streetscapes.
- Create identifiable, attractive, and safe building entrances.
- Enhance the visual quality of development by selecting appropriate materials and colours.
- Encourage the use of materials that minimise impacts on the environment.
- Encourage development to provide artworks and public installations that integrate into broader development and planning of Centres and contribute to the Centre's identity and sense of place.
- Ensure public plazas are located along popular pedestrian streets and near transit stops and are designed to allow for diverse functions, including supporting recreational activities, where space allows (Figure 9).
- Ensure plazas are designed to accommodate use in a variety of weather conditions.
- Ensure signs and advertisements contribute positively to the public domain and achieve a high level of design quality.
- Ensure that visual and physical amenity are not adversely impacted by visual clutter associated with the proliferation of signs.
- Ensure signs are clearly visible without dominating buildings, streets, or public places.
- Ensure signs and advertisements do not create a safety risk or hinder direct movement in high volume pedestrian areas.
- Provide signage for the purpose of wayfinding.

## 2.9.2 Controls


### Landscaping

- Development must incorporate landscaping to:

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 30 -->

- improve streetscapes by adding visual interest and soften the appearance of built form.
- promote safety and surveillance.
- attract and enhance urban biodiversity.
- provide tree-canopy cover to meet 40% canopy cover targets.
- minimise urban heat island effect ( Figure 7).
- break up the bulk and scale of development.
- complement the views to and from the public domain, as well as public and private open spaces.
- improve pedestrian comfort protect ion and enjoyment by providing shade, cooling, protection from wind and rain, improved air-quality, and access to the calming benefits of direct access to nature.
- Landscaping and providing deep-soil planting areas is to form an integral part of the overall design concept (Figure 8). A general requirement is to provide 40% landscaping. Council will assess landscaping provision on a merit basis that considers the proposed land use and detailed design quality.
- Footpaths shall be well-shaded and paved in materials consistent with Council's Engineering Design and Construction Specifications.
- Paving and other hard surfaces shall be consistent with architectural elements.
- Street tree planting shall be consistent with Part 3 Neighbourhood and Subdivision Design, and Part 8 Sustainability and Biodiversity of the DCP.
- Landscaping within car parking areas is to also comply with Section 4.11 Landscaping of car parking areas in this part the DCP.
- If planting areas are identified over basement parking, a minimum deep-soil planting zone of 3m 3 must be provided.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 31 -->

- Landscaping along the road shall not restrict driver sightlines to pedestrians, cyclists, and other
![page 31 img 1 (625x390)](images/wilton-development-control-plan-2021/p0031-b446b6a8fe36.png)


*Figure 7. Street landscaping to maintain sightlines and minimise urban heat island effect.*

vehicles at crossing points.

![page 31 img 2 (958x620)](images/wilton-development-control-plan-2021/p0031-aeb4ea42fd2e.png)


<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 32 -->


### Pedestrian connections, pedestrian crossings, through-site links, public spaces, plazas, and open spaces

- Where provided, pedestrian connections, pedestrian crossings and through-site links must:
- have a minimum width of 8m
- be at ground level and lined with active uses
- connect streets or lanes and have a clear line of sight between entrances and the public domain
- be direct and accessible
- be open to the sky and allow for adequate natural sunlight (Figure 10)
- be easily identifiable by users and include signage identifying the publicly accessible status of the link and the places to which it leads and connects
Figure 8. Landscaping and deep soil planting to provide tree canopy and enhance biodiversity.

- be clear of obstructions or structures and provide a clear sightline from one side to the other
- provide protection from wind and rain
- provide 24-hour public access, including safe night-time walking in areas of night-time activity
- include quality street furniture (including seats with arm rests and adjacent space for wheelchair users) and lighting
- Any subsequent development within a Centre is to maintain and enhance public spaces and should aim to increase the area of public spaces and pedestrian links.
- Outdoor spaces for the use of restaurants and cafes that are designed to activate the public domain will be supported in appropriate locations. Attention is also drawn to the provisions of the Local Government Act 1993 which authorises Council to grant a lease or licence of a footpath for restaurant purposes.
- Public art is to be provided in accordance with Council's relevant adopted policy.
- Public art and installations should respond to and build on the Centre's sense of place and identity.
- Create plazas that are in level with the pedestrian footpath and safely connected to the street.
- Plaza spaces are to be planted with trees, and installed with lights, drinking fountains, and both movable and fixed seating (Figure 9).

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 33 -->

- Make plazas accessible to bicyclists and provide bicycle parking facilities nearby (Figures 10 & 11).
- Where a raised or sunken plaza is necessary due to existing topography, design and provide ramps and/or stairs that are visible and accessible to all users.
![page 33 img 1 (331x328)](images/wilton-development-control-plan-2021/p0033-329af366d812.png)


*Figure 9. Public spaces are lined with attractive uses and provide high quality outdoor furniture and unobstructed sightlines.*


<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 34 -->

![page 34 img 1 (217x252)](images/wilton-development-control-plan-2021/p0034-21457024ce49.png)


*Figure 10. Public plazas and pedestrian connections add to the accessibility of the street pattern.*


### Active frontages

- Development is to provide active frontages that contribute to the liveliness and vitality of streets by maximising entries or windows to shops, food and drink premises, customer-service areas and activities that provide pedestrian interest and interaction (Figure 11).
- Development is to provide active frontages which are consistent with the relevant approved Neighbourhood Plan and Urban Design Concept Plan for the Centre. Additional active frontages can be proposed.
- Signage should not obscure visual access through glass shopfronts, to promote safety and maximise surveillance.
- Active frontages are to include a minimum of 75% of transparent glazing free of advertising or openings with a predominantly unobstructed view from the adjacent footpath to a depth of 6m within the building.
- The sill height of openings on the front facade of buildings is to match surrounding sill heights and be no more than 1.2m above the adjacent/abutting street paving. Translucent/tinted glazing or films are not permitted above a height of 1.2m from the footpath level.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 35 -->

- Solid non-transparent roller shutters are not permitted. Where premises require security grills or screens, they are to be located behind the glazing line, aim to minimise visual obtrusiveness, and maintain visual access into and out of the premises.
- Ground-floor uses are to be at the same level as the footpath at the entry to the individual tenancies (every 5-8m), except where development is required to comply with any floodplanning level provisions. Split-level arcades or open retail forecourts at a different level to the footpath are not supported, as they separate the activities within them from the street.
- Individual shops and uses along an active frontage are recommended to have an average frontage width of 5m to 8m. Where longer frontages are considered appropriate, they are to be limited to two within 100m of building frontage and be separated by at least one use with a frontage less than 8m.
- Retail active frontages are to have:
- a glazing line adjacent to the public domain, recessed entries may be appropriate to provide for a traditional-style shopfront.
- a minimum of 12 tenancy entrances per 100m.
- Building design at lower levels is to maintain a human scale through articulation and detailing (Figure 11).
![page 35 img 1 (397x297)](images/wilton-development-control-plan-2021/p0035-3d6c34ee72f9.png)


*Figure 11. Active frontages provide an engaging pedestrian experience.*


<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 36 -->


### Corner sites

28. Buildings on corners must address both frontages to the street and public spaces to:

- articulate street corners by massing and building articulation, to add variety and interest to the streets.
- present each frontage as a main street frontage.
- reflect the architecture, hierarchy, and characteristics of the streets they address, and align and reflect the corner conditions.
- Development on corner sites may require land that accommodates a splay corner to facilitate improved traffic conditions.
- Add additional height to buildings on corner sites through the integration of vertical elements such as raised parapets, spires, roof sections and similar structures.
- Consider integrating public art and signage into the design of buildings and related public spaces at prominent corner sites.

### Signage and wayfinding

- Signage is constructed of high quality and durable materials that can be maintained neat, clean, and tidy manner.
- Signage does not include moving, revolving, strobing, or flashing components.
- The illuminance, luminance and threshold increment of illuminated signage complies with Australian Standard 4282-1997 .
- Up-lighting of signs is prohibited.
- Any external lighting of signs is:
- Downward pointing.
- Focused directly on the sign.
- Prevents or minimises the escape of light beyond the sign.
- The maximum night-time luminance of any sign does not exceed 300cd/sqm. A lighting report may be required in some circumstances.

# 2.10 Community facilities


## 2.10.1 Objectives

- Maintain and enhance the existing streetscape and landscaped character.
- Ensure that development defines and enhances the public domain and desired street character.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 37 -->

- Ensure that building elements integrate with the overall building form and facade design.
- Ensure that any proposed developments within newly emerging areas respect and support the desired future character.

## 2.10.2 Controls

- Community facilities are to be designed and landscaped to enhance the quality and visual amenity of the streetscape and be sensitive to the streetscape character, adjacent uses, and buildings as well as views.
- The front entrance of all community facilities shall be in clear view of the street.
- Where a community facility has a dual frontage, the development shall be designed to address both streets, by way of windows and architectural features, and provide opportunities for passive surveillance.
- Where community facilities are proposed in predominantly residential areas or lower order Centres a minimum of:
- 25% of the site area shall be landscaped area.
- 50% of the front setback shall be landscaped area.
- Residential areas and lower order Centres require a minimum 1m landscaping strip between side setbacks and the driveway. The Landscape Plan is to demonstrate that the chosen landscaping species can adequately screen the development from the adjoining neighbour. If the Landscape Plan cannot demonstrate this, then additional setback area will be required.
- Landscaped areas in employment areas and other areas which are not predominantly residential areas shall be assessed on merit but shall achieve the 40% canopy target.
- A DA for the purposes of establishing a new community facility, intensification of an existing community facility or conversion/adaptation of existing buildings to a community facility must include an Operational Plan of Management. This will be used both for the assessment of the DA and help manage the ongoing operation of the proposed premises through the conditions of development consent. This plan must include, but is not limited to, the following:
- operating hours, including time allocated for cleaning and maintenance.
- the maximum number of staff.
- details of available public transport links, hub, and frequency.
- a safety audit and its recommendations.
- details of CPTED principles to be implemented.
- the nomination of a contact person that will respond to any issues or complaints that Council or the community raises.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 38 -->

- the measures that will be put in place to ensure that the amenity of the surrounding area will remain.

# 2.11 Mixed-use development

Read this section in conjunction with Section 4 Movement Network of this part of the DCP.


## 2.11.1 Objectives

- Provide for appropriate distribution and integration of different land uses within developments.
- Deliver activated human-scaled street frontages with good physical and visual connections between buildings and public spaces.
- Establish appropriate scale, dimensions, form, and separation of built elements to provide solar access and privacy.
- Achieve articulated built form and finishes that contribute to a high-quality and sustainable urban environment.
- Provide a high-quality public domain to achieve desired employment outcomes.

## 2.11.2 Controls

- Mixed-use developments should allow for a range of non-residential uses. Where the location or site constraints do not suit retail uses, the design should accommodate other uses, such as commercial offices.
- Non-residential uses should be on lower levels of buildings in areas where residential use may not be appropriate or desirable, such as along main roads or railway lines.
- Residential levels of the building should integrate with the development and maximise safety and amenity for residents.
- Residential uses within mixed-use developments must also comply with State Environmental Planning Policy No 65 - Design Quality of Residential Apartment Development (SEPP 65), the Apartment Design Guide and Part 4 of this DCP 'Development in Residential Areas.'
- Mixed-use developments should be provided in appropriate locations and deliver active street frontages that encourage pedestrian movement and vibrancy.
- Mixed-use development should concentrate around areas of high amenity, public transport, and centres.
- Residential, commercial, and retail uses on the upper floors are to be designed to overlook streets and public places to provide casual surveillance ( Figure 9).

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 39 -->

- In mixed use developments, provide a minimum floor to floor height of:
- 4m within podium levels in mixed use developments.
- Above the podium, if the use is residential accommodation, then the floor to ceiling heights are to comply with the ADG.
- In a mixed-use development, the provisions for podium and tower elements shall be as follows:
Podium

- Zero side setbacks are provided for the podium where residential uses are not provided on the ground level.
- Zero front and rear setbacks are provided unless stated otherwise in the Neighbourhood Plan.
Tower

- A primary street setback between 3m and 6m is provided. A rear setback of 3m to 6m is provided unless stated otherwise in the Neighbourhood Plan.
- A residential tower above the podium can have an increased setback away from main streets so that residential towers can have generous ground floor or landscaped courtyards.
- Confine signs to the ground level of the building, awning, or fascia unless demonstrated that the mixed-use building is of a scale, architectural style and in a location that would be enhanced by signage at different elevations.
- Mixed-use developments positively contribute to the public domain and must:
- address the street.
- provide activated frontages.
- provide for diverse activities and uses.
- avoid blank walls.
- consider live-work apartments at ground level as well as traditional commercial uses.
- provide good access, clear paths, and entrances, well-lit at night, a sense of safety
- Residential circulation areas should be clearly defined. Design solutions may include:
- residential entries separated from commercial entries and directly accessible from the street.
- commercial service areas separated from residential components.
- residential car parking and communal facilities separated or secured.
- security at entries and safe pedestrian routes.
- elimination of concealment opportunities.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 40 -->

- Landscaped communal open space should be provided at podium level, roof level or both.
- Where a food and drink premises are within a mixed-use building with residential units, impacts from internal transmission of noise, smoke and odour must be assessed and adequately managed.
- Provision of space within a new mixed-use development for vertical exhaust risers to service future ground-floor commercial uses must be included. Kitchen-exhaust air intakes and discharge points must comply with the requirements of Australian Standard 1668.2-2012 .

# 2.12 Out of Centre

The purpose of this section is to ensure that business and retail uses outside of nominated Centre and employment precinct supports the overall hierarchy of Centres and does not detract from the primary role and function established in the Hierarchy of Centre section of this DCP.

It is not the intention of this section to prevent development such as neighbourhood, café, or home business.

The aims of the approach to allow some business outside of the identified Centres is to reduce trips by private vehicles for minor trips, ensure the community is well connected and provide alternate locations to engage with the community that does not result in travel to an identified Centre.


## 2.12.1 Objectives

- Support a viable and successful hierarchy that consolidates investment in Centres within the Wilton Growth Area.
- Establish the Wilton Town Centre as the primary retail, community, and entertainment Strategic Centre within the Wilton Growth Area.
- Providing local access to services that enhance the pedestrian and active transport experience.
- Provide alternative convenience to local communities within close proximity of place of residence.
- Out of centre development should encourage local patronage.
- Establish planned centres as the appropriate location for supermarkets in line with Wilton Commercial Centres Strategy.
- Out of centre development does not detract from viability of the local towns and Wilton Town Centre.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 41 -->


## 2.12.2 Controls

- Minor retail development, such as neighbourhood shops, cafes or small takeaways, may be supported outside of Centres, provided they:
- Support the economic role of the planned centres.
- Result in a net community benefit, including:
- activation of the public domain.
- convenient walkable locations.
- appropriately mitigate land use conflict with surrounding land uses.
- The neighbourhood shops shall primarily be access via pedestrians and active transport. Limited parking is to be available at these locations.
- The overall Neighbourhood Centre will have a maximum size of 2,000m 2 to 3,000m 2 .
- Such shops can consist of newsagents, cafes, small conveyance/grocery shop, specialty food retailers and the like.
- The types of shops shall be complementary to the surrounding development and have minimal impact to the residents.
- Public spaces are accessible and do not interfere/conflict with active transport network.
- Supermarkets and large floor plate retail development should be located in centres.
- Suitable material choices that attenuate noise and smell and other impacts on surrounding areas.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 42 -->


# 3 General industry and employment uses (non-centres)


# 3.1 Application of this section

This section aims to guide the assessment of general industrial, light-industrial, warehousing, logistics, business park, specialised retail premises uses, sex services and restricted premises and car showrooms.

Read this section in conjunction with Section 4 Movement Network of this part of the DCP.


## 3.1.1 General principles and objectives

- Implement the objectives of the State Environmental Planning Policy (Precincts - Western Parkland City) 2021 (SEPP (Precincts - Western Parkland City)) and relevant Precinct Structure Plans.
- Allow for the appropriate subdivision of land.
- Promote quality outcomes for employment-generating uses outside centres.
- Ensure that any development does not unreasonably impact residents and the community.
- Prioritise health and wellbeing for workers and visitors within developments.
- Facilitate employment generation and maximise the potential of employment- generating industries.
- Encourage sustainable and environmentally responsible design.
- Encourage design of a type, scale, height, bulk, and character that enhances the streetscape character of the surrounding area.
- Allow for the design and uses of employment lands to adapt and change over time.
- New developments are planned and constructed to contribute to the social, environmental, and economic sustainability of Wilton and its surrounds
- Recognise the unique landscape and aesthetic qualities of Wilton and promote landscapeoriented development and best practice outcomes.
- Design and care for public space in a way that people of all ages and abilities can enjoy being there, with dignity and ease. Create clear entrances and exits, open areas and make it easy to understand how to move around and between spaces.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 43 -->

- Integrate public space with nearby land uses and public transport. Ideally, people should be able to easily get there on foot or by bike.

# 3.2 Lot subdivision

The subdivision of land, including strata subdivision, must support employment generation and must not inhibit the delivery of new essential infrastructure.


## 3.2.1 Objectives

- Allow for a range of lot sizes that cater for diverse land uses and employment opportunities within the Wilton Growth Area.
- Ensure the orientation and alignment of lots can provide for buildings to appropriately address streets and public spaces.
- Ensure lot sizes and street frontages can support the desired building type and use and achieve internal layouts appropriate to their function.
- Retain some large sites to accommodate large-scale businesses, such as business parks.
- Ensure natural topography and physical characteristics of the land is adhered to in order to minimise the need to cut and fill.
- Encourage active transport within employment subdivisions as well as to and from residential areas.

## 3.2.2 Controls

- Lots must be regular in shape with an orientation and alignment that enables future buildings to face the street and optimise solar access.
- Lot sizes and shapes within a large subdivision shall be diverse to meet a range of future land uses.
- Irregular-shaped allotments with narrow street frontages should be avoided. Lots shall be orientated and aligned:
- so future buildings can face arterial, sub-arterial, collector and local streets:
- to increase visual surveillance.
- to avoid streetscapes with loading docks and long blank walls.
- to facilitate solar efficiency.
- to encourage building design that has ecological areas, landscaped areas, and riparian corridors (Figure 12).

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 44 -->

- Sites for access to lots should ensure unimpeded sightlines for vehicles.
- Subdivisional roads shall incorporate a road hierarchy that will accommodate the anticipated traffic volumes and vehicle types and be practical and legible for all users.
- Where a residual lot is created, the applicant must demonstrate that future development of that residual lot can meet the controls in this DCP to ensure that the lot is not isolated from future employment-generating uses.
- Subdivisions shall include off-road provisions for walking and cycling on every internal and external street.
![page 44 img 1 (341x226)](images/wilton-development-control-plan-2021/p0044-7edc3c6f181b.png)


*Figure 12. Visible and direct interface to the public realm and connects to the natural system.*


### Minimum lot size

- There is no minimum lot size. The subdivision of land must meet the design controls within Part 4 Subdivision of the DCP.

### Minimum lot width

- Torrens Title subdivision of industrial land allotments (other than battle-axe- shaped allotments and residual lots) shall have a minimum width at the front building line of 35m. The minimum frontage within cul-de-sacs will be considered on merit and must be fully documented within the relevant Neighbourhood Plan.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 45 -->


### Battle-axe lots

- Battle-axe-shaped allotments are not supported (e.g. Figure 3), unless the applicant can suitably demonstrate that complying frontages cannot be provided as part of a Neighbourhood Plan, subdivision plan or both.
- Where it can be demonstrated that development requires a battle-axe shaped allotment, the following controls apply:
- battle-axe shaped allotments shall comply with the minimum lot width stipulated below, exclusive of the area taken up by the battle-axe handle.
- battle-axe handles shall have a minimum width of 8 m, with a minimum concrete accessway of 7m. However, where two battle- axe handles adjoin and provide a shared driveway with reciprocal rights of way over the other, the minimum width of the shared driveway may be 10m. there shall be a maximum of two lots per battle-axe handle.
- land within battle-axe handles that does not serve as a vehicle or pedestrian carriageway must be landscaped.
![page 45 img 1 (641x437)](images/wilton-development-control-plan-2021/p0045-655143c5bd45.png)


*Figure 13. Battle axe lot dimensions.*


### Strata or community title


<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 46 -->

- Within a strata or community title subdivision, parking spaces and spaces used for other purposes, such as storage, that are associated with an individual unit must be included in the same strata allotment as the unit.
- Visitor car spaces and loading spaces are to be designated as common property in a strata subdivision.
- Landscaping, communal open space, access areas, service areas and directory board signage, where not part of an individual unit in a strata subdivision, must be designated as common property.

### Landscape design at subdivision stage

- Proposals for all new business parks, industrial complexes and subdivisions must also have a landscape plan. If buildings are not part of the application, applicants must provide an indicative layout of buildings, building footprints, hardstand surface and deep-soil planting areas, watersensitive urban design elements, and access and car parking arrangements that comply with the relevant Neighbourhood Plan.
- Street tree planting at the subdivision stage is encouraged to ensure plantings are visually consistent in height, spread and form across employment-generating areas.

# 3.3 Building form and layout


## 3.3.1 Objectives

- Ensure the form and scale of development enhances the streetscape and visual quality of the area.
- Encourage innovative, contemporary, and sustainable building design.
- Achieve attractive streetscapes by ensuring that buildings present an acceptable scale and bulk when people view them from the public domain.
- Provide setbacks that integrate with adjoining land uses and that complement the desired future character.
- Ensure sufficient space for landscaping, onsite parking of bicycles, mobility vehicles and vehicles, access, and circulation.
- Encourage materials that minimise impacts on the environment, including embodied energy, biodiversity impacts and thermal efficiency.
- Ensure that any reflective materials are used with sensitivity to neighbouring development, vehicular traffic, and public domain areas.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 47 -->

- Ensure building materials mitigate noise impacts to adjoining development, particularly residential areas.
- Provide for quality landscaping and deep-soil planting areas within developments to deliver treecanopy cover, minimise the urban heat island effect, soften the visual appearance of the built environment and to enhance the working environment for employees.
- Prioritise the health and wellbeing of employees and visitors by providing well designed spaces that encourage casual social interaction.

## 3.3.2 Controls


### Setbacks

Setbacks play several important roles in employment-generating areas. Front, side, and rear setbacks ensure space for landscaping, contribute to streetscape consistency, and modulate building bulk and scale. Setbacks also provide a transitional area or buffer to adjoining land uses and ensure building entrances are clearly visible and minimise the impact of overshadowing to adjoining buildings and open space.

- All buildings are to be set back from the front property boundary according to distances set out in the Neighbourhood Plans.
- All setback areas shall be suitably landscaped and maintained.
- Hardstand areas within landscaped setbacks are to be minimised.
- Landscaped areas must allow for maintenance and security.

### Height

- Refer to the relevant Precinct Structure Plan for prescribed maximum building heights.

### Entrance treatment

- Entries to buildings should be clearly visible, well sign posted and appropriately lit for pedestrians and motorists.
- Development must provide architectural features at ground level to signify entrances and address the primary street frontage.
- All entrance treatments, such as directory boards, must be on private property, with appropriate positive covenants and restrictions on title to ensure the ongoing management of such treatments.
- Third-party advertising will not be permitted on any entrance treatment.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 48 -->


### External building materials and colours

- External finishes should consist of durable, high-quality, and low-maintenance materials.
- External finishes should contain a combination of materials and colours.
- Any wall visible from the public domain must be finished with a material that enhances the façade.
- Building materials must minimise reflection.
- The following matters should be considered in choosing building materials to ensure that the materials contribute positively to ecological sustainability and minimise energy consumption:
- energy efficiency.
- use of renewable resources.
- maintenance cost and durability.
- recycled or recyclable materials.
- non-polluting materials.
- minimal PVC content.
- building lifecycle, including ongoing maintenance and operational efficiencies.
- Where development proposes concrete roofs for additional parking, parapet edges with appropriate screening to conceal rooftop car parking are mandatory.
- Materials used for roof construction and external walls should use materials with low heat absorption to reduce the urban heat island effect.
- Materials likely to contribute to poor internal air quality and those containing volatile organic compounds are not permitted.
Applicants must submit a materials and colour sample schedule detailing external colours and finishes with all DAs.


### Landscaping

- Front and side setbacks must have landscaping to soften and screen buildings, storage, service, and parking areas.
- Landscape design should integrate with site planning and building design to:
- reduce the perceived scale of built form from the street.
- reduce visual impact and the extent of continuous building facades.
- highlight architectural features and complement facade articulation.
- identify site and building entries, car park entries and parking areas, in coordination with signage.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 49 -->

- mitigate adverse site conditions by buffering western sun, providing shade and wind protection, and screening poor views.
- maximise northern sun exposure.
- integrate usable and attractive external seating and amenity areas for staff, incorporating paved areas, soft landscaping, and shade planting (and canopies where necessary).
- Landscaping and fencing should not obscure building entries and sightlines.
- A minimum of 20% of the site shall be deep-soil landscaping that can support plants including trees. The provision of landscaping will be assessed on a merit basis that considers the proposed land use and detailed design quality.
- The development must provide deep-soil landscaped areas at the front of the site and in communal areas (Figure 14).
- Deep-soil landscaped areas shall have a minimum dimension of 3m 3 to ensure that large shrubs and trees can be supported.
- The design and location of security fencing must minimise visual impacts and integrate with the landscaping design. The location of security fences behind landscaped setbacks.
- All landscaped areas must have an automatic irrigation system, preferably underground, to ensure they can be efficiently maintained.
![page 49 img 1 (566x388)](images/wilton-development-control-plan-2021/p0049-f8ebee9f8c76.png)


*Figure 14. An example of deep soil planting.*


<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 50 -->


### Communal areas

- Each development must provide at least one communal area for employees and visitors to use and enjoy. The area should be suitably sized, landscaped, and accessible from the main office component of the development.
- Communal areas must be outside and must include both soft landscaping and deep-soil planting.
- Small pockets of open space designed to enhance the appearance of the development will not be counted in the communal area allocation, nor will areas for car parking, manoeuvring or landscaped setbacks.
- In locating communal areas, consideration should be given to the outlook, natural features of the site and neighbouring buildings.
- Communal areas shall be embellished with appropriate landscaping to provide shade in summer and sunshine in winter, weather protection, paving, tables, chairs, and the like.
- Communal areas shall be relatively flat and not contain impediments that divide the area or create physical barriers that may impede its use.
- Communal open spaces must have solar access. Communal areas must receive a minimum of two hours of direct sunlight between 11am and 3pm on the 21st of June every year.

# 3.4 Public domain (streetscape and public spaces)

Developments for a range of employment-generating uses should enhance the streetscape by addressing the street and providing points of interest. There should be a balance between built form and landscaped elements.


## 3.4.1 Objectives

- Encourage innovation and a high standard of architectural design, utilising quality materials and finishes.
- Activate streets and the public domain with building frontages.
- Provide a variety of building orientations and create defined streetscapes that respond to site conditions.
- Ensure that building design enhances the existing and future desired built form character by encouraging innovation and quality architectural design.
- Enhance the visual quality of development by selecting appropriate materials and colours.
- Create identifiable, attractive, and safe entrances to buildings.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 51 -->

- Embed circular economy design principles to maximise recycling and reuse of materials.

## 3.4.2 Controls

- Blank building facades facing the primary street frontage are not permitted.
- The built form and architecture of buildings on street corners shall enhance its location and positively respond to and emphasise the street corner.
- Building orientation and siting shall respond to natural elements such as topography, wind, and sunlight.
- Buildings shall provide effective sun shading for windows, wall surfaces and building entries (other than loading docks) using design elements such as overhanging eaves and awnings, under crofts, colonnades and external sun-shading devices including screens.
- Building design shall integrate with landscape elements.
- The bulk and scale of the building shall maintain significant views and vistas. Building facades are to be articulated and varied by:
- highlighting building entries within front facades.
- including upper storey windows.
- emphasising street corners.
- featuring external structures, finishes, etchings, and recessed patterns.
- including decorative features, textures, and colours.
- emphasising customer entries and service-access doors.
- including protrusions and penetrations in building elements, such as upper storeys that project over entries and breaks in the built form.
- Building design must consider the amenity of any landscaped or communal areas in adjoining properties.
- The location of roller shutters, loading docks and other building openings should not detract from the overall appearance of the building. Roller shutters and the like should not be on the primary street frontage.
- Roof design is to be visually interesting, provide for natural lighting and be compatible with the overall building design. Where visible from a street or public space, all rooftop, or exposed structures (lift motor rooms and plant rooms, for example) must be suitably screened and integrate with the building.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 52 -->


# 3.5 Fencing, signage, and lighting


## 3.5.1 Objectives

- Use fencing to define boundaries, provide security, and contribute to streetscape and amenity of the area.
- Enhance pedestrian safety, security, and amenity within the area.
- Ensure that fencing, signage, and lighting support the visual appearance and safety of developments and the visual appeal of the area.
- Ensure that fencing, signage, and lighting do not discourage people from walking and cycling.

## 3.5.2 Controls


### Fencing

- Low feature walls should be designed at the entry point of driveways. They should integrate with the overall design of the development. Where appropriate, they should be incorporated as retaining walls, garden beds or landscaped features.
- Front and side boundary fences in front of the building line shall consist of an open palisadestyle fence finished in a dark colour.
- Side fencing behind the building line should be open-style fencing, such as chain- wire fencing, finished in a dark colour to promote casual surveillance.
- Fencing should be located and designed to maintain sightlines for pedestrians and drivers.
- Fencing to front boundaries should be between 1m and 1.8m in height, measured from finished ground level.
- Fencing to side and rear boundaries should be between 1.8m and 2.5m in height, measured from finished ground level.

### Signage

- Signage must relate to the use of the property and should identify the relevant business name.
- Business identification signage should be attached to the wall of the main building and be designed to complement the architectural style of the building.
- Freestanding signs will only be permitted where signs integrate with the landscaping and visual character of the site and surrounding area.
- Directional signs for car parking areas, loading docks, delivery areas and the like should be located close to the main access of a development site.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 53 -->

- Signage must only display corporate logos and company names and must not occupy more than 10% of any facade or wall of a building unless it can be demonstrated that characteristics of the site or the building require larger signage.
- Signage must not have a negative impact on the visual character of the site or surrounding area.
- Details of all signage, including freestanding, fascia, and wall signs, must accompany DAs.
![page 53 img 1 (916x546)](images/wilton-development-control-plan-2021/p0053-eaba935f2376.png)


*Figure 15 Various styles of signage attached to building walls.*


### Lighting

- The design and lux of any internal or spot lighting shall be designed to avoid off-site or traffic safety impacts.
- Moving or flashing signage or lighting is not permitted.
- Lighting design should address the principles of CPTED where there is significant pedestrian activity, late night work-shifts or safety and security issues.

# 3.6 Ancillary buildings, storage, and service areas


## 3.6.1 Objectives

- Promote integrated design for auxiliary buildings, site facilities, storage and service areas that deliver quality streetscapes.
- Ensure that site facilities are functional, accessible, and easy to maintain.
- Minimise the impact of service access on pedestrian movement and industrial, commercial, and retail frontages.
- Minimise the visual and acoustic impact of site servicing.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 54 -->


## 3.6.2 Controls

- The location of ancillary buildings, site facilities, storage and service areas should be considered at the initial design phase.
- Ancillary buildings, storage areas and site facilities should be located behind setback lines and be consistent with the design of the main building.
- Ancillary buildings, site facilities, storage and service areas must integrate with the overall design, be visually and physically unobtrusive, and not adversely impact the streetscape.
- Details of any proposed ancillary buildings, open storage and services areas must be submitted with all Das, including access arrangements, and managing noise, odour, and safety issues.
- Storage areas should be located within the primary building and have appropriate screening.
- Above-ground open-storage areas visible from the public domain are not permitted.
- Signage and management strategies must be put in place to ensure that storage activities do not impact or extend into landscaped areas. Storage is not allowed in landscaped areas.
- Developments must provide rubbish and recycling areas that comply with council requirements. These areas must:
- integrate with the development
- minimise the visibility of these facilities from the street.
- be located away from openable windows to habitable room.
- provide barrier-free access to all shared facilities.
- Outdoor storage areas may be considered by Council on merit. Where developments propose these, the following information must be provided at the DA stage:
- size of outdoor storage area.
- maximum storage height.
- types of goods, materials and equipment being stored.
- details on landscaping and screening structures.
- evidence that the proposal does not compromise truck or vehicle manoeuvring or car parking areas.
- Above-ground water tanks must not be in front of the main building line or visible from the public domain. Tanks must have suitable screening. Details (including elevations and screening) of all water tanks with DAs.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 55 -->


# 3.7 Specialist retail premises


## 3.7.1 Objectives

- Ensure that the design of specialised retail premises contributes positively to the streetscape and public domain with high-quality architecture, materials, and finishes.
- Establish requirements for specialised retail premises, including minimum size of individual tenancies and ancillary retailing.

## 3.7.2 Controls

- An individual tenancy within a specialised retail premises must have a sales floor area of greater than 500m 2 accessible to the public.
- Cafes and fast-food restaurants are only permissible in specialised retail premises tenancies that have a sales area of larger than 2,000m 2 accessible to the public. Cafes and fast-food restaurants within such tenancies must not be more than 150m 2 .
- Specialised retail premises must be designed to:
- address and activate street frontages with large display windows.
- define and enhance the public domain.
- have setbacks consistent with surrounding development.
- be in scale with surrounding buildings.
- provide facades and built form with varied and interesting architecture, especially for visually prominent building locations, such as lower. level front facades, rooftops and street vista terminations
- avoid ambiguous external spaces with poor pedestrian amenity and security.
- provide clearly identifiable and dedicated pedestrian access to the building and across the site from the primary street frontage.
- create a visually interesting place for pedestrians and, where possible, enhance pedestrian and cycle networks and connections to surroundings.
- provide active uses that address the street and can be seen from the public domain.
- Design must be flexible to cater for different future uses by providing high ceilings and adaptable open planning for the ground and first floor.
- Buildings must be close to the street alignment and designed so that key operational spaces are legible from the street.
- Parking is not permitted between any street frontage and the building.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 56 -->

- Where an allotment has frontages to a classified road and a local road, the development should provide vehicle access from the local road.
- Developments must provide pick-up areas to avoid the need for customers to carry large items to vehicles.
- Developments must provide public toilets in a specialised retail premises at the minimum rate specified under the National Construction Code.
- A specialised retail premises must provide at least one independent non-gender- specific parent room.

# 3.8 Sex services premises

This section applies to sex services premises as defined in the SEPP (Precincts - Western Parkland City).


## 3.8.1 Objectives

- Provide certainty for both the consent authority and the local community about the ongoing management practices the proposed use will use to manage its impact on the neighbourhood.
- Ensure that sex services premises aim to minimise their potential impacts on the area.
- Ensure that the design and external appearance of the premises and any associated structures have no negative impact and are in keeping with the character of the area.
- Ensure premises aim to address the safety of workers, visitors and the public.
- Ensure that sex services premises operate at times where they will have the least impact on the community, the environment and nearby land uses.
- Ensure that signage is discreet and in a suitable location for sex service premises and that it considers the amenity of the surrounding area.
- Ensure that signage does not result in visual clutter or other visual impacts on an area.
- Minimise the potential for signage to offend the public.

## 3.8.2 Controls

- A DA for establishing a new sex services premises, intensifying an existing sex services premises, or converting or adapting existing buildings to a sex services premises must include an operational plan of management. This plan will both inform the assessment of the DA and help manage the ongoing operation of the proposed premises through the conditions of development

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 57 -->

consent. This plan (including amendments, if required) must include, but is not limited to, the following:

- operating hours, including time for cleaning and maintenance.
- the maximum number of staff on the premises at any one time.
- the maximum number of sex workers on the premises at any one time.
- number of clients on the premises at one time.
- a statement detailing how premises will comply with health requirements.
- details of available public transport links, hub, and frequency.
- a safety audit and its recommendations.
- details of CPTED principles to be implemented. details must include information on the following:
- casual surveillance of exits and entries.
- lighting.
- landscaping.
- security.
- safe handling of money.
- the nomination of a contact person that will respond to any issues or complaints from council or the community.
- measures that will be put in place to ensure that the amenity of the surrounding area will be maintained.
- The exterior of sex services premises must respect the architectural character of the streetscape and not be a prominent feature in the street.
- All entrances and exits to sex services premises shall be designed to facilitate the privacy of staff and visitors without compromising personal safety (through avoiding the use of isolated back lanes and poorly lit areas). Shared access to the premises for both staff and visitors is not permitted.
- The interior of sex services premises must not be visible from any place in the public domain. Where the interior of sex services premises may be visible from neighbouring buildings, adequate measures shall be taken to screen the interior of the building-for example using blinds and screens.
- Sex-related products, sex workers or performers, or nude or semi-dressed staff shall not be visible from the public domain.
- The premises must have a waiting area of adequate size within the premises to prevent people from waiting outside the premises.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 58 -->

- Development of a sex services premises must not occur if it is within 200 m of the boundary of an existing legally operating sex services premises.
- Council will exercise its discretion relating to permitted hours of operation of sex services premises by considering the nature of adjoining land uses, the hours of operation and use of those premises, and possible conflicts with such uses.
- Hours of operation shall comply with Section 5 Night-time economy of this part of the DCP.
- Council allows only one external sign per premises, which indicates only the name of the business, the address or both. Additional signage for parking and traffic management may be provided.
- Where primary pedestrian access is from the rear of the site or from a car park, the premises may provide a second sign at the rear entry of the site, indicating only the name of the business and the street number or address.
- The size of the sign must not exceed 0.3m x 0.6m (or other dimensions but of equivalent area of 0.18m 2 ).
- Illuminated or flashing signs are not permitted.
- The sign shall not display words or images that are, in the opinion of the consent authority, sexually explicit, lewd or otherwise offensive.
- The premises must display a clearly visible street number to avoid disturbing surrounding premises.

### Assessment principles

When assessing a DA under this clause, council must consider:

- Whether the impacts of the sex services premises would adversely affect the neighbourhood because of its size, location, parking arrangements, visual effects, and hours of operation.
- The cumulative impact of sex services premises, restricted premises, and other premises on the streetscape as well as the potential for creating an undesirable character or attracting antisocial behaviour to the area.

# 3.9 Restricted premises

This section applies to restricted premises as defined in the SEPP (Precincts - Western Parkland City).


<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 59 -->


## 3.9.1 Objectives

- Ensure that the development of restricted premises does not adversely affect the existing and future amenity of surrounding development, land uses and residents.
- Ensure the location of restricted premises is away from land uses that their operation may adversely affect.
- Limit the cumulative impact of several restricted premises and other related premises in a single area.
- Protect the character of an area or streetscape by controlling displays, advertising, and signage.

## 3.9.2 Controls

- Development of a restricted premises must not occur if it is:
- within 50m of any form of residential accommodation (excluding the Wilton Town Centre), open space or land occupied by a sensitive land use. Sensitive land uses include, but are not limited to, places of public worship, churches, schools, transport nodes, residential dwellings, childcare centres, family health centres, senior citizens centres, licensed premises (licensed under the Liquor Act 1982 ), and facilities and places that families and children typically frequent.
- within 100m of the boundary of an existing legally operating restricted premises.
- on the ground floor of retail or commercial premises.
- within immediate proximity of the residential entrances of a mixed development.
- located where it may conflict with the residential amenity of a mixed development.
- All restricted premises must comply with the requirements of the Crimes Act 1900 Section 578 (e) and Classification (Publications, Films and Computer Games) Enforcement Act 1995.

# 3.10 Car showrooms


## 3.10.1 Objectives

- Ensure that car showrooms aim to minimise their potential impacts on the area.
- Ensure that the design and external appearance of the premises and any associated structures have no negative impact and are in keeping with the character of the area.
- Ensure that car showrooms aim to address the safety of workers, visitors and the public.
- Ensure that all signage is of high-quality design and material and in a suitable location and that it considers the impact on the surrounding area, particularly any illuminated signage.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 60 -->

- Ensure that signage does not result in visual clutter or other visual impacts on an area.

## 3.10.2 Controls

- Council will assess all car showrooms on merit. The key matters which must be satisfactorily addressed by any proposal for a car showroom (whether new, refurbishment or change of use) are as follows:
- Site coverage which must not exceed 70%.
- minimisation of prominent vast areas of parked cars, in this regard any second-hand cars for sale in the form of a 'car lot' is limited to a maximum of 20 cars.
- the need to increase landscaping measures to address and mitigate impacts, such as planted buffers or permeable paving.
- appropriate fencing design. Building design must:
- provide high ceilings and adaptable open planning for the ground and first levels.
- provide for a perimeter-type built form that addresses street frontages.
- allow for proximity to the street alignment and ensure that key operational spaces are legible from the street, with large display windows.
- be scaled to define the street and to relate appropriately to surrounding buildings.
- have continuous uniform setbacks with surrounding development.
- incorporate details and interesting architectural features, especially at visually prominent locations, such as lower-level front facades, rooftops and street vista terminations.
vii. avoid ambiguous external spaces that may hinder pedestrian safety and security. viii. contribute to and enhance public open space and public streets.

- create a visually interesting space for pedestrians and where possible, enhance the pedestrian networks and linkages.
- Cars are to be displayed within the building to enable the development of the built form along the perimeter building line.
- The storage of any vehicles should be within buildings and not visible to the public domain.
- Where development proposes external vehicle parking areas, these must incorporate landscaping and permeable or semipermeable surfaces.
- Signage must not cover windows or detract from the architectural quality of the building design or amenity of streets.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 61 -->


# 4 Movement network

This section sets out the policy approach, provision rates and technical design controls for parking and access in employment-generating areas. This section controls the location of vehicle access, vehicle-access design and design of other services to minimise impacts.


# 4.1.1 Objectives

- Encourage active transport and deliver a quality pedestrian experience.
- Encourage an appropriate mix of on and off-street parking options within mixed-use areas.
- Provide an appropriate number of parking spaces to service the needs of both employees and visitors.
- Provide integrated vehicle, bicycle and service access points without compromising streetscape character or the walkability of neighbourhoods.
- Ensure design of car parking as well as basement layouts and service areas maximise pedestrian safety and provides adequate space for manoeuvrability and creates high-quality ground-level relationships between the building and the public domain.
- Ensure parking areas incorporate dedicated parking bays for electric vehicle charging and facilities for e-bike and micro mobility charging.

# 4.1.2 Controls


## Commercial and mixed-use developments

- Development is to deliver adequate provision of secure bicycle parking (preferably including bike lockers or bike hangars), secure micro mobility parking (including charging points), end-of-trip facilities, including showers and lockers (coin operated/tap and go for shopping centres).
- Applicants must submit a Green Travel Plan for any office, business or retail development that exceeds 3,000 m 2 gross floor area (GFA) or accommodates more than 50 employees. A Green Travel Plan must include:
- targets, including reduction in single-occupancy car trips and increased mode trips for sustainable transport.
- travel data, with baseline travel demand and mode share estimates derived from experience with similar developments.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 62 -->

- The location and design of vehicle access for parking and loading as well as the location and design of building services must not diminish the commercial street vitality, visual character or pedestrian safety.
- Locate vehicular access points away from active pedestrian areas on secondary streets or lanes and away from any active frontages. Ensure access points have adequate sight distances and ensure that vehicles can safely enter and exit the site.
- The area dedicated for car parking, loading and services on the ground floor that relates to the street front must be minimised so that a viable commercial floor area is provided to accommodate a variety of commercial uses.
- Below ground (basement) car parking is required for developments with large street frontage widths, for higher density residential and mixed-use blocks as well as within Centres.
- Above ground parking is only acceptable if it is designed to mitigate all adverse visual impacts.
- Garage doors must not encroach on a public footpath during operation.
- Any commercial customer car parking spaces must be conveniently located, identified as such and directly accessible to the general public (that is, not behind a security grill or gate) during opening hours. Commercial customer car parking must be secure outside opening hours.
- Any residential visitor car parking spaces must be conveniently located and identified as such. it must be either fully accessible outside security measures or accessible through a building intercom system at the vehicle access entry.
- Except as required for commercial customer car parking or residential visitor parking, all other car parking and loading areas must incorporate security measures to restrict access.
- Mixed-use developments shall be designed to locate driveways, carports or garages away from bedrooms.
- Car parking vents must not to be located on building frontages.
- Building services must be accommodated at the rear, within a basement or, where essential, within lobby areas, discretely designed to minimise the loss of active frontage commercial area and visual impact to the streetscape.
- Open parking areas and access ways must be suitably landscaped to enhance amenity.

## General industrial, light-industrial, warehouse, logistics, business parks and specialised retail premises type uses

16. No parking is permitted in the landscape setback.

- All vehicles are to enter and leave the site in a forward direction. The design of parking and servicing areas is to:

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 63 -->

- enable uninterrupted two-way vehicle movement to and from the site where required by Standards Australia's 'Parking facilities-Off-streetcar parking' standard.
- slow vehicles to less than 30 kilometres per hour.
- separate parking and loading areas.
- provide for all loading to occur within the site.
- provide for all servicing, including garbage collection, to occur wholly within the site, with suitable collection points at convenient locations.
- provide separated pedestrian-access routes to the main entries to the building both from the public domain and within the site from parking areas.
- provide a separated pedestrian walkway for the main pedestrian flows from customer car parks with more than 50 car spaces.
- locate parking access ramps within the building footprint.
- Where shared access and turning areas are proposed, a single DA is required for all development which will use the shared arrangements, and it is to address how the land is to be subdivided and resulting easements.
- Design all major vehicular circulation, including strata industrial circulation, generally to be 'street like' in appearance and include a carriageway, kerbs, footpaths and tree planting.
- Minimise the visual impact of vehicular access and servicing areas on the public domain by:
- minimising the size of the vehicular access (width and height).
- setting roller shutters back from the street frontage of the building.
- providing landscaping to screen views to the roller shutter.
- Locate vehicular access points away from active pedestrian areas on secondary streets or lanes. Ensure access points have adequate sight distances and are design to ensure that vehicles are able to safely enter and exit the site.
- Minimise the width of driveway footpath crossings and maximise the width of pedestrian clear paths of travel.
- Sunken loading docks should be avoided.
- The design of specialised retail premises is to provide sufficient manoeuvring areas on site to accommodate large truck and bus movements, frequency of servicing and high turnover of customer vehicles.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 64 -->


# 4.2 Bike parking and associated facilities


## 4.2.1 Objectives

- Support cycle movement over the use of private motor vehicles.
- Ensure that bike parking is considered in all development and provided in appropriately scaled developments with facilities such as change rooms, showers and secure areas for bike parking.
- Provide safe and secure cycle parking facilities in locations that reinforce its preference as an alternative to private motor vehicle use.
- Encourage bike parking areas incorporate dedicated parking bays for e-bike charging.

## 4.2.2 Controls

- All development is to provide on-site bike parking designed in accordance with the relevant Australian Standards for the design criteria of bike parking facilities. Reference should be made to Australian Standard AS2890.3 - Bicycle Parking Facilities (as amended) and Austroads's Guide to Traffic Engineering Practice - Part 14 - Bicycles.
- Bike parking spaces for new development are to be provided in Table 3. The minimum number of bike parking spaces is to be rounded up to the nearest whole number if it is not a whole number.
- Development is to consider appropriate distribution of dedicated off road cycleways, and offroad shared paths connecting key destinations, centres, and employment areas.
- Bicycle storage facilities shall be designed to be capable of accommodating and supporting all usual types of bicycles, and to minimise damage in storage or during movement in and out of the parking space.
- Secure bike parking facilities are to be provided in accordance with the following:
- class 2 bike facilities for staff/employees of any land use.
- class 3 bike rails for visitors of any land use.
- Where bike parking for tenants is proposed in a basement, it is to be located:
- on the uppermost level of the basement.
- close to entry/exit points.
- subject to security camera surveillance where such security systems exist.
- A safe path of travel from bike parking areas to entry/exit points is to be marked. Access to bike parking areas must be:
- a minimum of 1.8 m wide to allow a pedestrian and cyclist to pass each other and may be shared with vehicles within buildings and at entries to buildings.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 65 -->

- accessible by a ramp.
- clearly identified by signage.
- accessible through appropriate security or intercom systems.
- Bike parking for visitors is to be provided in an accessible on-grade location near a major public entrance to a development and is to be sign posted.
- The following facilities for bike parking are to be provided at the following rates:
- One personal locker for each bike parking space.
- One shower and 1 changing cubicles for as many as 10 bike parking spaces.
- Two showers and changing cubicles for 11 to 20 bike parking spaces.
- Two additional showers and changing rooms for each additional 20 bike parking spaces or part thereof.
- developments may provide shower and changing facilities in the form of showers and changing cubicles in a unisex area in both male and female changing rooms.
- lockers, changing rooms and shower facilities must be close to the bike parking area and entry and exit points and must be within an area of security camera surveillance where such building security systems are in place.

# 4.3 Bus parking


## 4.3.1 Objective

- Accommodate temporary bus parking associated with specific land uses.

## 4.3.2 Controls

- Where the proposed land use is a hotel, factory outlet store or another use that attracts persons arriving or leaving by bus, the DA is to include a Parking and Access Report which assesses the provision of bus set-down and parking.
- Parking spaces for buses are to be provided in addition to other parking requirements.

# 4.4 Car parking rates


## 4.4.1 Objectives

- Encourage people to travel to and from employment areas by walking, bicycling, public transport, micro mobility device or motorcycle, and to travel by car only as a last resort.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 66 -->

- Ensure all land uses or combinations of activities provide sufficient parking onsite to satisfy the demand for parking by different vehicle types generated by the development.
- Minimise reliance on street parking.
- Minimise conflict between pedestrians and vehicles at the junction of driveways and footpaths.
- Minimise potential impacts of driveway locations on streets and intersections.

## 4.4.2 Controls

- Safe and secure car parking shall be provided in accordance with the car parking requirements set out in Table 3.
- Where the car parking requirement is expressed as a minimum number of spaces, the development shall not provide less spaces than that minimum.
- When the calculations for the numbers of parking spaces results in a part or fraction of a parking space of 0.5 or greater for the whole development, then the actual number shall be rounded up. For example, 1.5 spaces shall be rounded up to 2 spaces for the whole development.
- Where a development proposal contains two or more land uses the parking requirement shall be the sum of parking required for the individual land uses. However, where the proposed land uses comprise different peak parking demands, the total requirement may be reduced such that the peak demand is met at any one time, where supported by a study by a suitably qualified traffic engineer.
- Where car parking requirements for a proposed development are not listed in the DCP, or where the development proposal raises unique traffic and parking issues, or where development is identified as a 'traffic-generating development', applicants must complete a Traffic Report.
- A Traffic Report is also required for a 'traffic-generating development' as defined by State Environmental Planning Policy (Transport and Infrastructure) 2021 .
- Developments are to provide accessible car parking to meet current standards.
- A credit /offset system to reduce the required number of car parking spaces can be applied to a development if:
- a Parking and Transport Study or Parking Supply and Management Plan is provided that supports the reduction.
- the provision of additional infrastructure that supports wider community use of alternate modes of transport is proposed within a development is supported by a parking strategy for that development.
- shared parking can be enabled between areas such as schools, parks, and shops and this shared use is supported by a parking strategy.

<!-- SRC: 01-wilton-growth-area-development-control-plan-part-9.pdf | PAGE: 67 -->

- developments are designed to incorporate adequate pick-up/drop off zones to enable use of ride share/taxis/public transport on demand and the like.
